Prisoner. I was going down Holborn—a boy asked me to buy these books, and said he wanted some money to buy bread—I said I had no money, but I would give him some bread, which I had just got, and I did so—I was going down Hand-court with the books, and the officer took
me—the baker came to the prosecutor's shop, and said I had had two pounds of bread of him.
JOHN PIPER ( police-constable.)I took the prisoner as he was running—I asked what he had in his apron—he said, "Nothing"—I found these books—he said he had given two pounds of bread for them—the baker came to the prosecutor's shop, and said he had received two pounds of bread that morning, which is the Parish allowance.
ALEXANDER SMITH . I am the prisoner's brother. He lost his father, and was in the workhouse—the master turned him out, and gave him 6 d.—since then he has had the allowance of bread from the Parish.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350202-628]
628. DANIEL DRISCOLL was indicted for stealing, on the 5th of February, 9 1/4 lbs. of sugar, value 6 s., the goods of Thomas Lewis.
THOMAS LEWIS . I live in King-street, Lower-road, Islington, and am a grocer. This is my sugar—my name is on the wrapper—I left my shop, on the 5th of February, a few minutes after nine o'clock, and never saw the sugar again, till it was at the station.
HENRY WINGROVE . I live in Queen-street, Islington. About eleven o'clock that morning, I was about a dozen doors from the prosecutor's, and saw the prisoner and another boy lurking about the shop—the other boy went into the shop, brought out the loaf of sugar, and gave it to the prisoner, who put it into his apron, and walked away—Mrs. Lewis came out, and said, "Wingrove, stop him."
Prisoner. The other boy went in, and came out and dropped the sugar on the edge of the door—it rolled down to the kennel—I did not have it at all—I hardly know what dress the other boy had on. Witness. they were together for five minutes before he went in—I was loading dung—Mrs. Lewis took up the sugar—I took the prisoner—he kneeled down and begged for pardon and mercy—my horse was towards Mrs. Lewis's shop—I watched them, I may say, ten minutes—the prisoner appeared to be waiting for the other while he went in—my partner caught the other boy, but he was let go.
GUILTY . Aged 12.— Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18350202-629]
629. JOHN RUSSELL was indicted for stealing, on the 28th of January, 40 yards of flannel, value 2 l. 6 s., the goods of Thomas Savage.
THOMAS SAVAGE . I placed this roll of flannel at my door on a box, which was about a foot inside, and half a foot outside the house, about nine or ten o'clock in the morning of the 28th of January—I went out about three o'clock in the afternoon, and returned at five o'clock—I then heard a piece of flannel had been stolen—this is it—it has my mark on it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. What mark have you on it? A. The private cost mark and the selling mark—I bought it of Llewellin and Co. in Aldermanbury—the marks are my own writing—I live in Tottenham-court-road.
JOHN KNIGHT ( police-constable F 105.) I saw the prisoner with this in Broadway, St. Giles's—he had it under his arm—I watched him some distance—he saw me, and ran away directly—I ran and took him.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18350202-630]
630. WILLIAM TINDELL was indicted for a misdemeanor.
WILLIAM ROBERTS . I live on Old Fish-street-hill. I am a dry-salter and
colour manufacturer—the prisoner came to me early in January, (I had known him before,) and said he wanted some samples of colour—he said he could sell a great quantity of colour—I said I should be very glad if he could, and asked in what way he proposed to sell them—he said, "I will sell the colour for you, Sir, and give you an opportunity of receiving the account; or I will give you security to the amount of 500 l. "—I said I would rather decline his selling, and my receiving the account; I would prefer his security—he came again on the 10th of January, and wanted a sample of blue, at about 6 1/2 d. per pound—he said he should want 5cwt. for Messrs. Wrench and Co. London-wall—I gave him a sample, and told him, that of that quality, I had only one cask, which was about 2cwt. and a quarter—he went away—I received this note about two o'clock, but I do not know the prisoner's writing—about four o'clock, a man came for a cask of blue which Mr. Tindell had ordered—that was the substance of the note—I had spoken to the prisoner about this cask, and I kept it till two o'clock, at his request—I refused to deliver the cask at four o'clock to the man who came—he waited, and in about a quarter of an hour the prisoner came, and asked me to give him the weight of the cask, which I did—it was 233lbs.—he said he wished I would make up a paper parcel of 35lbs. more, which would make up 2cwt. and a half—I told him I could not, I had no more of the same description—he then stated, that he had not got the bill in his pocket—I considered this blue was for himself—he had mentioned the house of Wrench and Co. as the house to whom be had sold it.
Q. What was the false pretence which induced you to part with this? A. He had talked to me about giving me security, and I was desirous of having security—he said he had given Wrench fourteen days credit, and he would give me an order upon Wrench and Co. to receive the money in fourteen days—I understood I was to have the order at that time when he had the blue—it was in consequence of that statement I gave him the blue—he stated, he had not got the bill in his possession, but he could get it in twenty minutes; and as it was getting very late, it being then half-past four, he would feel obliged if I would allow the man to take the cask to Wrench and Co.'s, as he believed they were desirous of shipping it that night—he had at first proposed that I should go to Liverpool to see his uncle—I declined that, and he then laid he would give me a bill for 18 l. on his intended father-in-law.
Q. Was it in consequence of the statement about the order that you gave him the blue? A. He said he would return in twenty minutes, and would then give me the order on Wrench and Co.
JURY. Q. Was that an order for you to receive the money instead of the prisoner? A. Yes, that was what I understood.
COURT. Q. Did he return in twenty minutes, having had the blue? A. No, he did not—he asked me at the time the blue went out of my premises, to allow him a halfpenny a pound, which was what he should get by it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When you were examined on this matter before Mr. Alderman Kelly, were you not asked whether you had sold the prisoner goods on credit previous to this? A. Yes; he had had some trifling articles—I believe I stated that—I did not say that he had not had them—he said he had sold this blue to Wrench and Co., and I let him have it, believing he had sold it to them; but I should not have parted with it, only that he said he would give me the order—I gave him credit, believing he would return in twenty minutes, and give me the order—he
had promised me the 18 l. bill before, but I did not receive it till seven days afterwards, on the 17th of January—the cask of blue was 6 l. 7 s. 11 d.—he had before that had goods to the amount of 1 l. 6 s.
Q. Just look at this note, and tell me when you sent this to the prisoner? A. It is dated Thursday evening; I believe that was on the 15th, five days after this transaction. ( Reads)—"I am desirous that you should call upon me, and pay for the goods you have had, or else give such reference as will give satisfaction. Punctuality is the very hinge of business."
Q. And on the 17th he sent you a bill for 18 l.? A. Yes; but I had not had the order—he had goods after the blue transaction, but I did not know how this was—he did not come to me after he had had the blue—he wrote me a plausible letter on the 14th of January—the 18 l. bill would more than cover the blue transaction and the other, but I am not satisfied with the bill—I do not think it is good—it is not due yet—if it should be paid, I shall take legal advice to know whether I might deduct the money for the blue.
FRANCIS BISHOP . I am clerk to Wrench and Co. I manage their bills—I am generally employed in the counting-house—I never authorised the prisoner to draw upon them for any money—I have never paid any draft he has drawn—he has never drawn any—he has never given us credit for fourteen days—we never had any goods of him—if he ever had authority to draw upon us, I must have known it—I never authorized him to get a cask of blue, nor have Wrench and Co.—he did not send us in a cask of blue on the 10th of January—there is no truth in any part of the statement.
Cross-examined. Q. Is Mr. Wrench here? A. No—there is no other clerk there—a gentleman was assisting at the time in arranging the affairs of the late firm of Turner and Wrench—that firm was put an end to on the 31st of December, 1833—Mr. Wrench could not give any orders in town; he was at Newcastle-upon-Tyne at the time—I bought goods at that time—I have not the books of the house here—Mr. Jacob Wrench is the only person in the business—it goes by the name of Wrench and Co., but there is no partner.
WILLIAM DAGGET . I am clerk to Messrs. Fothergills. My employer had a cask of blue, on the 10th of January, of a man named Henderson—it came a little after four o'clock—we gave 42 s. a cwt. for it—it came to 4 l. 7 s. 4 d.—it weighed 233lbs.—Mr. Roberts has seen the same cask.
ME. ROBERTS . I have no doubt it is the same cask.
CHARLES TRIMMINGS . I was hired by a man named Henderson, who was with the prisoner, to draw a cask, which he said was coming from Mr. Roberts, of Fish-street-hill—he hired me down by the iron bridge—I took the cask from Mr. Roberts' to Fothergills'—I went up Fish-street-hill—I could not get up, and the prisoner helped me—I met Henderson at the corner of Little Trinity-lane—he told me to follow him, and he led me to Fothergills'.
GUILTY . Aged 21.—See Kent Cases, page 668.
[trialAccount t18350202-631]
631. DANIEL SUDDEN WOOD was indicted for a misdemeanor.
JOHN VALENTINE . I had my pocket picked in Drury-lane, on the 27th of January—I secured that person, and the prisoner came up and struck me a violent blow on the nose, but before he came up, while I
had hold of the other boy, who was George Brown, there was a cry of "Dan," "Dan"—Brown and the other persons called out so, and then the prisoner came up and struck me—there was a cry of, "Go it, Dan, give it him"—I had my hat knocked off, and some men came up with baskets and struck me—the officer came up, and the prisoner was secured.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. What are you? A. A bar-man. This was about nine o'clock in the evening—there was a crowd round us before the prisoner came up, but I had dragged the boy who had robbed me, across the street, because the people wanted to get me down a street—there were all sorts of characters round me—when the prisoner came up the mob was pushing me about—I am quite sure they did not push me against the prisoner—I am sure I did not go against him—I had my eye on Brown, but when I found I had tight hold of him, I looked for a officer—I was not very much confused—Brown has been tried here this morning—I am quite sure the prisoner struck me—he might have been a second in front of me before he struck me—he had time to see his companion, if he was his companion, but they had not been in company then—this was last Tuesday week—I had a swollen nose.
WILLIAM DUNGATE ( police-constable F 22.) I was on duty, and saw the mob—I saw the prosecutor detaining a boy, and the prisoner struck him a violent blow on the nose—I did not hear any cry then—I afterwards heard the cry of, "Go it, Dan."
(—O'Grady, of Charlotte-street, and—Taylor, of Charlotte-street, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350202-632]
632. WILLIAM LUPTON was indicted for that he, on the 2nd of January, feloniously did forge an order for payment of money, which is as follows:—"London, January 25.—Messrs. Herries and Co., Pay to M. S. Chifney, or Bearer, the sum of 500 l.
RICHARD TATTERSALL" —with intent to defraud Sir Thomas Harvie, Farquhar, and Co., against the Statute.—2nd COUNT, for offering, uttering, disposing of, and putting off a like forged order, with a like intent.—Two other COUNTS, calling it a warrant for payment of money.—4 other COUNTS, stating his intent to be to defraud Richard Tattershall.—8 other COUNTS, the same, only omitting to set out the forged instrument.
MESSRS. BODKIN and DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
SAMUEL COLEMAN . I am porter at the New Hummums, Covent-gardon. On Friday, the find, of January, the prisoner came into the hall at the Hummums, a few minutes before three o'clock—he had a green shade over his eyes, and a cloak on, which resembled this one—he inquired for Major James—I said I did not know Major James—he said he was going to dine there—I said I did not know him, but very possibly he might be coming to dime there—he then said, "I want a porter to walk with me a little way, I have something for him to do"—I went out with him—we went down Tavistock-row, and down Bedford-street—when we got to the bottom the prisoner went into Mr. Webb's shop, at the corner of the Strand and Bedford-street, and he desired me to wait outside—he came out again with a letter in his hand, and crossed over to the other corner of Bedford-street—he held the letter up, and directed me to go with it—he said, "Take this to No. 16, St. James's-street, and wait an answer, and I shall be at the Hummums"—this is the same letter, I have every reason to believe
—( looking at it)—I went to No. 16, St. James-street—I found it to be the Banking-house of Herries, Farquhar, and Co.—I went in and gave the letter to Mr. Hance, one of the clerks—I was taken into a back parlour where some gentlemen sat—they asked me from whom I brought the letter, and I told them I brought it from a gentleman, but I did not know his name—there was some conversation between them, but I cannot tell what, and I was allowed to come out—when I came out, I saw the prisoner looking at me very intently, and watching me—he was facing the door in St. James-street—I had my hand in my pocket, and walked on—the prisoner followed me round into King-street—I noticed him very much, and was almost positive he was the man that gave me the letter—I went back, and stated my suspicions to the clerks at the banking-house—there was some time elapsed, and Mr. Hance then came out with me—we went up to St. James-square, and saw the prisoner on the right hand side of the square, nearly facing No. 6, on the Pall-Mall side—I tapped him on the shoulder as he was walking gently on—I said, "I beg your pardon, but I believe you are the gentleman who gave me a letter from the Hummums, and took me to the bottom of Bedford-street—he said, "No, I think you are mistaken"—when I heard his voice, I was almost positive he was the man—Mr. Hance then came up and spoke to him, and asked him to walk back to the banking-house with us—we came back together, Mr. Hance taking hold of his arm, and I walked a little behind, just behind the prisoner, as close as I could walk—as we were going along, Mr. Hance said, "He has thrown something down, pick it up"—I saw a person walking behind me, and he picked up a green shade—he was holding it in his hand—I said, "I beg your pardon, give that to me, it belongs to this gentleman"—I might have said, "I beg your pardon," I will not be positive—the person gave me the shade—this is it—I do not know whether the prisoner heard what I said about the shade—he might have heard it—I do not know that he did—this shade is very similar to the one the prisoner had over his eyes when he came to the Hummums.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I thought you said it was about three o'clock when the person you call the prisoner came to the Hummums, did you observe the clock? A. I said a little before three o'clock, it perhaps might want ten minutes—our people were dining at the time, and I was in the hall—there is a clock in the hall—I had looked at it—I should think it was about seven minutes before three o'clock—I could not say to the moment—the cloak and shade rather concealed the person—the shade concealed his eyes, and the cloak concealed his person and dress in some manner—I could see his legs, he had boots and trowsers on—I believe his trowsers were blue—he had a hat on—the shade projected a little beyond his hat, it being over his face—I could see the bottom portion of his face—I have no doubt I should know the man if I saw him in Yorkshire a year hence—he has a particular voice.
Q. You said, before he spoke, and after he had spoken, that you were almost positive he was the man—I suppose you mean you were not quite positive? A. When I heard him speak I was quite positive—the person who came to the Hummums seemed lame, and leaned on my arm—when we saw the prisoner in St. James's-square he was not walking lame—I believe the man is not here who picked up the shade, he seemed a working man—I saw him pick it up, where it came from I cannot tell—I saw him stooping and picking it up, when Mr. Hance told me the prisoner had dropped something—I did not inquire who he was—I did not ask his name
and address—the prisoner walked slowly from the Hummums to the baker's shop, leaning on my arm.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Do you know any thing of the lameness more than that he appeared to be so? A. No.
COURT. Q. You say he walked slowly? A. Yes, and pressed on my arm—he said he was lame—it was his own observation—at that time he had a cloak and a shade on—he had no stick.
Prisoner. Q. When you saw me in the Square did you not say you thought that was the man from his hat and boots? A. I did not say so there—I have said so.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Where aid you say that? A. I do not recollect where, but I did not say it there—my memory is not bad.
Prisoner. At the station-house, when they asked you whether you could not swear to my voice, did you not say, "No, the person was so muffled up when he spoke?" Witness. No, I have no recollection of it—I will not swear I did not—I might have said it and forgotten it.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Did you notice the hat and boots of the person? A. Yes, the hat was bent in behind.
Prisoner. This is the hat I had on ( putting it on.) Witness. I believe it is.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Did not you, at the police-office, insist upon the hat, and cloak, and screen being put upon the man? A. I did not—they were put on him.
COURT. Q. Did that increase to diminish your opinion that he was the same man? A. I was convinced he was, when I saw him dressed in the same way as the man was who addressed me at the Hummums—I first remarked that his hat was bent behind when he walked arm in arm with me—I am taller than him, and I noticed it; and I saw the same bend in the hat when I saw him in St. James'-square—I cannot say that I made that remark before the magistrate—I might have said it.
MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Was what you said read over to you? A. Yes, I signed it—I cannot call to mind whether I mentioned about the bend in the hat.
HENRY HANCE . I am clerk in the banking-house of Messrs. Herries, Farquhar, and Co.—Sir Thomas Harvie Farquhar is one of the partners, and he has more than one partner—Messrs. Tattersalls keep cash at our house—on the 2nd of January, between three and four o'clock, I was at the counter—this witness came in, and brought this letter—upon opening it, I found it confined the draft—I suspected it was not genuine—I asked Coleman in, and he was asked some questions—we were satisfied with his account, and let him go—in the course of a minute or two he came back and made a communication which induced me to go out, and I saw the prisoner standing at the corner of King-street and St. James'-street, with a red pocket-handkerchief in his hand—I went out at our private-door in King-street, not the door that Coleman went out at—the prisoner seemed to be watching the door of the banking-house in St. James'-street—I went round to Coleman—the prisoner had then left the corner, and was going in the direction of St. James'-square—we went in that direction, and overtook the prisoner in St. James'-square—Coleman accosted him, and said he was the party who had sent him with a letter to St. James-street—the prisoner said, "Not me, Sir"—Coleman persisted in it, and said he was positive he was the man—I asked the prisoner to return with me, and I took hold of his right arm—I asked him
if he had no better employ than sending porters to banking-houses—he said, "I do not know any thing about it, it was not me"—he then proceeded in the direction of the banking-house, and I asked where he had been for the last ten minutes—he said, "Making a call in King-street, at No. 10, but the house was shut up"—I observed, that was a gambling-house—he said it was, or "Yes"—I asked if he knew Mr. Chifney—he said he knew two gentlemen of the name of Chifney—he wished to disengage himself from my hold, and said there was no occasion for that.
Prisoner. That was in the square—when you took hold of me, I said I would walk without your holding me. Witness. We then proceeded towards the banking-house—Coleman walked with us abreast—the prisoner put his right hand into his breeches pocket, and was feeling about—some time after he drew it out, and I saw something on the pavement, on the spot which we had passed immediately before—I think it was about twelve yards from us—I had not noticed any thing when I had passed that part of the pavement.
COURT. Q. Had it so happened that you had passed and repassed the same spot? A. Yes; but that was some minutes previously.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What did you do? A. I told Coleman to pick it up—I believe I said "He has dropped something, pick it up"—a person took it up, and was in the act of fixing it on the palisades of a house, and Coleman took it off—the person gave it up instantly to Coleman, who has retained it ever since—I told Coleman the prisoner had dropped something, and told him to pick it up, in a tone of voice that the prisoner could hear, but he said nothing.
Cross-examined. Q. Did the prisoner walk very lame? A. Not at all, Sir—when I took him by the arm, he told me to let him go, he could walk by himself—when I saw him in King-street, he was walking up and down, but not going from the corner—he spoke to one of the men who were at work, doing some repairs at Crockford's, which is called St. James's Bazaar—that is not No. 10—I believe No. 10 is a gaming-house—he might have been there before I came out—he walked with me to the banking-house—I asked him if he knew Major James, and he said he did not—the officer was sent for—the prisoner's residence was not asked in my hearing till he was at Marlborough-street—he gave it without any reservation, and I believe it was correct—I did not attend particularly to where it was.
HENRY SHERSBY . I am in the employ of Mr. Webb, No. 422, Strand, at the corner of Bedford-street. On the 2nd of January, a person came into the shop about three o'clock in the afternoon—to the best of my belief, it was the prisoner—he had a brown cloak on, and a green shade over his eyes—I have no doubt in swearing that this is the cloak—I have seen but one like it in town—my master deals in such articles—I did pot notice the colour of the lining—I cannot say whether this is the shade—when he came into the shop he asked me which was the way to Pall-mall, and I told him—he stood in the shop some little time—I did not notice that he had any thing in his hand—when he left our shop I saw him in conversation with Coleman, at the other corner of Bedford-street, by Mr. Ward's, the baker—they then separated, and the prisoner went into Mr. Ward's.
Cross-examined. Q. What peculiarity is there in this cloak? A. It is a peculiar colour, a reddish brown—I believe the prisoner to be the man.
AUGUSTA WARD . I am the daughter of Mr. Ward, a biscuit-baker, at the corner of Bedford street. On the 2nd of January, I was in the shop,
about three o'clock in the afternoon, a person came in and asked to leave a cloak—he took it off, and laid it on the counter—he said, "Will you allow me to leave this till I return from Charing-cross?"—I did not take notice of the person—I cannot tell whether it was the prisoner or not—the policeman afterwards came, and had the same cloak, about nine o'clock in the evening—the gentleman who was to return from Charing-cross never came for it.
COURT. Q. Did you remark the size or general appearance of the person? A. No, my Lord, I did not take any notice of him; but from what little I saw of him, I should say the prisoner is the man—he was in the act of taking the cloak off as he came into the shop—I was in the parlour, and came into the shop.
Cross-examined. Q. I believe you took no particular notice of the person? A. No.
ELIZABETH WARD . I am sister of the last witness. I recollect, on the 2nd of January, a person coming into my father's shop—I was in the parlour behind, but I could see into the shop very plainly—I saw the person taking a cloak off—I should not know him.
RICHARD TATTERSALL . I am proprietor of the Subscription-rooms, at the corner of Hyde-park. This cheque is not my handwriting, and I never authorized any person to write it—I do not keep a private account at Herries'—we keep the account of the firm there—I have known the prisoner some years—I should call him a leg —he lives by betting—he was in the habit of frequenting our rooms—I have seen him frequently—I have seen him with a cloak on—I could not say as to the colour of it—I have seen him with a shade over his eyes.
Cross-examined. Q. He is a betting man? A. Yes—I think that is not right, but such persons resort to our rooms.
GEORGE STONE ( police-constable C 99.) On the 2nd of January, I went to Mr. Ward's shop, and received this cloak—the prisoner told me he lived at No. 12, Lascelles-place, Bloomsbury—I found this pocket-book and paper there.
GEORGE HOBBS ( police-constable C 85.) I searched the prisoner's lodging, at No. 18, Lascelles-place, and I found some papers, which I sealed up.
( The cheque was put in and read.)
RICHARD TATTERSALL re-examined. There is a jockey very well known, of the name of Chifney. On the envelope, inclosing the cheque, was written, "St. James'-street, No. 16, a cheque, please to send it short"—the sheets of paper, found at the prisoner's lodgings, were the same in size and appearance as the one which had inclosed the cheque, and had the same water-mark; and some slips of paper were found in the pocket-book, the same in size and appearance as the one on which the cheque was written.
Prisoner's Defence. I know nothing of the circumstance, any more than being on the spot in the square—I seem confounded—I can only say I do not know any thing of it—I have nothing to say particularly.
MARY TONBRIDGE . I live at No. 12, Lascelles-place, Broad-street, Bloomsbury. The prisoner lodged with me last month—he certainly was at home on the 2nd of January—I did not see him at all that day myself—my daughter saw him at three o'clock that day.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Have you seen him with a cloak? A. Yes.
JURY. Q. What sort of a cloak was it? A. I could not tell—I never was very near him when he had it on.
COURT. Q. How long have you known him? A. He lodged with me twelve months last October.
JANE TONBRIDGE . I live at home with my mother. The last time I saw the prisoner was on Friday—I do not know about how long ago it—I am eleven years old—it was on a Friday, about three o'clock—I asked my mother, because I had to do something for Mrs. Lupton, when Mr. Lupton was gone out—I asked my mother if he was gone out—she said she did not know; and I went up, and saw him sitting by the fire—he went out not long afterwards—I cannot tell how long.
MR. BODKIN. Q. Was this Mr. Lupton's cloak? A. I am sure I do not know—I was not at home when the officer brought this cloak to our house—I did not look at the clock, but my mother said it was three.
Prisoner. She went out at half-past two o'clock for some beer for me.
MR. PHILLIPS to MARY TONBRIDGE. Q. What day was it your daughter asked you this question? A. On the day Mr. Lupton was taken into custody—I know the time from my own eight-day clock, which stood in the parlour—my daughter went up stairs, but Mr. Lupton was not gone out.
MR. BODKIN. Q. What time did Mr. Lupton go out? A. I do not know—he was in the house at three o'clock, but after that I cannot tell—I should think my house is about ten minutes' walk from the Hummums in Covent-garden—the officers came to my house when I was not at home—they came a second time, and brought a cloak—they asked if I knew it—I said, it was very like Mr. Lupton's, but I would not swear to it, as there were so many alike.
GUILTY of Uttering. Aged 63.— Transported for Life.
ESSEX LARCENY.
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350202-633]
633. GEORGE TOLLEY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of January, 1 sack, value 1 s.; and 2 1/2 bushels of oats and beans mixed together, value 10 s.; the goods of Robert Wagstaff, his master; and that he had before been convicted of felony.
ROBERT WAGSTAFF . I am a farmer, and live at West Ham, in Essex. The prisoner had been in my service about seven months—he was a carter and ploughman—on the 17th of January, on my way home round my fields, I discovered a sack in a ditch of what we call the nine-acre field—the sack had my name on it, and contained beans and oats mixed—I had no doubt of the sack and its contents being mine, and I directed my servant, James Trench, to watch—the sack was covered with grass, as if to conceal it—I set Trench to watch on Saturday night, and he brought the prisoner to me between five and six o'clock on the Monday following—the prisoner said, "Master, your watchman has caught me with a little corn—I am very sorry for it—I meant to give it to my horses."
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. He said he had been caught with a little corn? A. Yes; I deal in potatoes—this was a potatoe sack—I found this sack in one of my own fields, forty-two rods from my house—there are no persons to whom I sell potatoes, nearer than the village, which is about sixty rods off—there was a small quantity of bran in this corn—
there was some bran in my granary, which lies south-west, and when the wind sets that way, it blows the bran over the corn.
JAMES TRENCH . I watched from the Saturday till the Sunday night, and the prisoner came between five and six o'clock, and took the sack out—I followed him—I then heard a cart coming—I drew back, and laid down under the hedge—the carter saw me, and he passed by without stopping—the prisoner came again at six o'clock and took the sack, (which he had before removed to a gate-way near the road,) and I collared him.
Cross-examined. Q. Must not the prisoner have seen you the first time? A. I do not know that he did—the carter saw me, and went past—I had no occasion to take the prisoner then, for I expected he would come back, and he did—I knew him—he did not see me the second time, till I took hold of him—I said, "George, I have got you"—he said he knew had had done wrong, and he was sorry for it—I did not say any thing to him about a pot of beer—he said he was going to take it to his master's horses, but he was taking it the wrong way to go to the stable.
CHARLES SMITH . I received charge of the prisoner and this sack—when I was searching him at the watch-house, I said, "George, it is a bad job for you"—he said, "Yes, I know I have done wrong, but I must go through it now"—he said he took the corn out of the bin, and it had been there for a week.
JOHN WRIGHT . I produce a certificate of the prisoner's conviction, which I got at Mr. Clark's office ( read)—I took him on that occassion, and I know he is the same man.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Fourteen Years.
KENT LARCENIES, &c.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350202-634]
634. MARY ANN WHITE and JANE JONES was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, at St. Paul, Deptford, Kent, 1 watch, value 3 l. 10 s.; 2 seals, value 2 l.; 1 watch-key, value 10 s.; 1 split ring, value 2 d.; and 1 watch-ribbon, value 4 d.; the goods of George Morgan, in his dwelling-house; and MARY MOOR was indicted for feloniously receiving the same, well knowing them to have been stolen, against the Statute, &c,—2nd COUNT. For receiving them of an evil-disposed person.
GEORGE MORGAN . I am a hair-dresser, and live at No. 6, Broom-field's, in the parish of St. Paul, Deptford, Kent. I occupy the house entirely myself. On the morning of the 10th of January, I met the prisoners White and Jones, in High-street, Deptford—my brother was with me at the time—I took them home to my house, and gave them some supper—I went into the shop with Jones, and while I was there my brother came into the shop and said something to me—he wished me to give him some money—I refused—Jones and I returned to the parlour—White and my brother were there—the women went away, and after they were gone I missed a watch which had hung over the mantel-piece in the parlour—I gave information to the police—I have since seen it in the possession of the policeman Collings—the prisoner Moor was never in the house, to my knowledge.
WILLIAM CRUTON ( police-constable R 151.) I was on duty in High-street, Deptford, on the morning of the 10th of January, about half-past one o'clock, or rather more—Mr. Morgan and his brother came and gave
me information about this—I went with Collings to the lodging of Jones and White—I found Jones at the door—White came in afterwards, in company with a girl named Turner—they denied all knowledge of the transaction.
WILLIAM COLLINGS ( police-sergeant R 18.) In consequence of information from Cruton, I went with him into New-street, Deptford—I found Jones in Crown-court there—I asked her how long she had been in—she said she had not been out that evening—I asked if she had been to any hair-dresser's shop—she said she had not—at that time White and Turner came in—I asked White the same question—she denied all knowledge of having been in the shop—I sent for Morgan, who identified White and Jones—I afterwards found a piece of silk at their lodging—they said they lived there with Turner—I found the silk lying on the floor—I afterwards found in a room in that street, this watch, wrapped up in two shawls in the possession of Moor—she said something as I went into the room—it is four doors from where the other prisoners live—on my entering the room she snatched up a bundle which lay on the floor—I immediately took it from her, and in the middle of two shawls I found this watch—I asked how she came by it, and all she said was, "I took it."
WILLIAM JAMES ( police-constable R 83.) I received information, and went to No. 2, Crown-street, New-street—I found Jones there with the police-constable and sergeant—I said to Jones, "There are three females live here"—she said the other two were gone away to the privy—I directly turned out of the house, and saw White coming from the house four doors off, where Moor lives—she was pulling the door to with her right hand—she came into Jones's apartment—we searched the house, but could find nothing.
WILLIAM COLLINGS re-examined. Neither of the prisoners were in the room I found the silk in—it was Turner whom we suspected—it was at White and Jones's room that they told me they rented the room—I asked each of them if they rented it, and they said the room belonged to them jointly.
GEORGE MORGAN re-examined. I can identify this piece of silk by frequently seeing it—I know it by the colour and the shape—I had such a piece in my watch—I was about five minutes in the room after I came from the shop with Jones—the women wished to go home—I was in the shop with Jones eight or ten minutes—we were both in the room about twenty minutes before that.
THOMAS MORGAN . I am the prosecutor's brother. I was in the room with White about ten minutes, or more—I asked George for some money, which he refused, and the women went away about five or ten minutes after—I was about five minutes in the shop with my brother—I had left White in the parlour, and directly I came into the shop Jones went into the parlour to White; they went away because my brother refused to give any money.
White. Thomas Morgan gave me the watch to satisfy me, as his brother had no money. Witness. Nothing occurred between me and White—I had half-a-crown and sixpence in my pocket—I went to get change from my brother—White hardly spoke to me—we could not agree—I gave her nothing, and had nothing at all to do with her—I did not give her the watch.
Jones's Defence. I saw Thomas Morgan give White the watch when I came into the parlour, leaving George Morgan in the shop.
Moors Defence. White brought a shawl to me, and asked me to mind it until the morning—I did not know what was in it.
GEORGE MORGAN re-examined. I gave 3 l. 10 s. for the watch two months ago—it is worth that—the seals are gold, and are worth 2 l. 10 s., and the gold key 10 s.
JURY. Q. Were you sober at the time? A. No; neither of us were sober—we had been drinking in the course of the evening—nothing occurred between me and Jones in the shop—I know nothing of the shawl.
WILLIAM COLLINGS . White claimed the shawl; the watch was found in—there were two shawls—Turner claimed the other.
WHITE — GUILTY of stealing under the value of £5. Aged 18.
Confined One Year.
JONES— NOT GUILTY .
MOOR— NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18350202-636]
636. WILLIAM SMAILL was indicted for stealing, On the 21st of October, 1 purse, value 1 d.; 1 half-sovereign, 2 half-crowns, 3 shillings, and 4 sixpences; the goods and monies of Thomas Button; and that he had before been convicted of felony.
THOMAS BUTTON . I am waiter at the Mitre Tavern, at Greenwich. On the 21st of October, I put a half-sovereign, two half-crowns, three shillings, and four sixpences, in a purse, in my coat pocket, between eight and nine o'clock in the evening—I hung my coat behind the door' in the pantry, and left it there all night—on the following morning, I went to look, and the money was gone—I told my master—he desired toe to keep it quiet, and he had no doubt we should find it—the prisoner was porter at the tavern, and in the evening we heard he was going to Charlton fair—my master said he would grant him leave to go—I-went after him, brought him back, and told him what had happened, and that I thought he had taken it—he denied it, and was a little saucy—I searched him, and took 2 s. 3 d. from him—my master started him, and in about two hours he came back, and gave me in charge to a policeman for robbing him of 2 s. 3 d., which he said was his money, and he would summons me next morning for it.
EDWARD AGAR . I was ostler at the Mitre Tavern. Of the Tuesday morning, I was in the stable-yard—the prisoner came, and I heard him go into the dust-hole, between seven and eight o'clock—the prosecutor told me, about twelve or one o'clock, that he had lost some money, and I went to the dust-hole, and found the purse and 15 s. in money in it—there was a half-sovereign, three shillings, and four sixpences—the two half-crowns were gone.
JAMES CHAMBERLAIN ( police-constable R 200.) I was on duty—the prisoner came to me and said he had been robbed of 3 s. 3 d.—I took him, but cannot find where the half-crowns have been changed.
THOMAS BUTTON . This is my purse—there was no one in the pantry with me, when I put the money in my coat, but the prisoner.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to Charlton fair, and was taken, but I never saw the money.
GUILTY .* Aged 18.— Recommended to mercy by the Prosecutor.
Transported for Seven Years.
[trialAccount t18350202-637]
637. EDWARD THOMPSON and SAMUEL WHITEHOUSE were indicted for stealing 80lbs. of rope, value 3 s., the goods of Charles Enderby and others.
HENRY TURNER . I am in the employ of Messrs. Charles Enderby and Brothers of Greenwich. On Michaelmas-day last, I fixed a quantity of rope round their premises in Greenwich-marshes—it was tied from tree to tree—it was old rope, which had been in use—I saw it safe on the Sunday before the policeman brought it—this is part of it—I know it by the splices and ties on it.
JONATHAN SHEPHERD ( police-constable R 105.) I was on duty on the 15th of January, about nine o'clock, and saw the prisoners in Church-street, Deptford—Whitehouse had all this rope—I stopped him, and Thompson came up, and took part of it, and said it was his property—that he had been on board a vessel lying off Greenwich, called the Effort, and bought it—and he was going to take it into Grove-street, to make door mats—Whitehouse said he had brought it from Woolwich, and was going to make mats of it—I took them.
Whitehouse's Defence. I was passing Greenwich College, and met Thompson, who said he would give me 6 d. to carry it.
Thompson. Whitehouse is innocent—I employed him to carry it.
THOMPSON— GUILTY . Aged 20.
WHITEHOUSE— GUILTY . Aged 17.
Confined One Month.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.

638. ELIZABETH EDDS was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 1 blanket, value 3 s.; 1 sheet, value 3 s.; and an iron pot, value 1 s. 6 d.; the goods of Thomas George.
MARY GEORGE . I am the wife of Thomas George, we live at Woolwich. I let a ready-furnished room to the prisoner, for which she paid 2 s. 6 d. a week—she lived there more than two months—she did not leave my house at all—on the 19th of January the went out in the morning—I watched for her the whole day, and when she came back, I told her I was informed she had stolen some goods from her room, and I asked her to give me the key—she said she would not—if she took the goods, what was that to me, if she brought them back—she at last gave me the key, and I went into her room with the constable—I missed a blanket and an iron pot—and on the Monday following I missed a sheet—she had paid me my rent all but one week—she said she was a bounty widow, and had 25 l. a year, and took in needle-work beside—I gave her liberty to go away at night, to bring the thing, next morning, which she said she would.
ELIZABETH HYDE . I live in Thomas-street, Woolwich, and keep a clothes shop. I bought a blanket of the prisoner, but it was not the one the prosecutor lost—I bought the duplicate of it the first week in January—I believe the pawnbroker is not here—the lanket was produced before the magistrate, but it was not the one the prosecutor lost.
CHARLES STEWART WARDEN . I am superintendent of the police at Woolwich. On the 19th of January, Mrs. George gave me information that she had lost these articles—I went to Mrs. Hyde, and found this pot and sheet.
ELIZABETH HYDE . I bought them of the prisoner—I think the latter end of December.
JURY. Q. What did you give for the pot? A. Eight-pence—I do not suppose it is worth more than 10 d. or a 1 s.—I gave her 6 d. for the duplicate of the sheet, which was pawned for 1 s. 6 d.
( Property produed and sworn to.)
Prisoners Defence. When I came home, there was a padlock on the door—I told her, what there was deficient I would replace, and she said she would willingly take them—she let me go that night, and when I returned, she said it was too late, and gave me in charge.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury. — Confined One Month.
[trialAccount t18350202-639]
639. WILLIAM HAWKINS was indicted for stealing; on the 15th of January, 1 cheese, value 2 s., the goods of William Waghorn.
WILLIAM WAGHORN . I am a cheesemonger, and life at Woolwich. On the 15th of January, at half-past eight o'clock in the evening, I was in my shop—I saw the prisoner come to the window, and take this cheese from a pile of twenty-eight cheeses—he ran away—I followed, and overtook him about a hundred yards off—this is the cheese—the prisoner is a marine, and is quartered at Woolwich.
GUILTY . Aged 22.— Confined Six Weeks.
[trialAccount t18350202-640]
640. SAMUEL BIRD was indicted for stealing, on the 10th of January, 1 pair of trowsers, value 16 s., the goods of William Oliver Henderson.
WILLIAM OLIVER HENDERSON . I am a clothier, and live at Woolwich. I lost a pair of trowsers on the 10th of January, which had been hanging on an iron rod, which goes into my shop and round the door-post.
JAMES CHANDLER . I am shopman to Mr. Davis, a pawnbroker. On the evening of the 10th of January, the prisoner brought these trowsers to me to pawn. I saw the legs were pinned together—I sent for the officer, and took the prisoner.
WILLIAM CHITTENDLE . I am an officer. I took the prisoner, and produce the trowsers.
GUILTY . Aged 32.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18350202-641]
641. WILLIAM BESSER was indicted for stealing, on the 29th of January, 1 pair of boots, value 3 s., the goods of Andrew Donohue.
ANDREW DONOHUE . I am in the artillery, and am quartered in Woolwich. On the 29th of January I got intoxicated, and lost a pair of boots which were on my feet—I cannot tell how they came off, or whether I took them off myself.
CHARLES DAWSON . I am a watchman it Woolwich. On the morning of the 29th of January, at two o'clock, I saw the prosecutor lying under the batchers'shambles in the old market-place—he had only one boot on, and no cap—I searched about and found his cap, but could not find his boot—I saw him again in a short time after, when I had been round my beat, and then his other boot was gone, and he was turned over—I took him to the watch-house, but could not find the boots.
WILLIAM DAVIS . I am a police officer. I saw the prisoner on the 29th of January, about twelve o'clock in the night, with these boots under his arm—I followed him about two hundred yards, and asked him what he had got—he said, "A pair of old boots"—I took them, and examined them—they appeared to be military boots—I took him to the watch-house, and there found the prosecutor—the prisoner said he found them in the market.
NOT GUILTY .
Sixth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

642. JANE WAILS was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of January,1 hat, value 6 s., the goods of William Whittingham; and 1 bonnet, value 5 s., the goods of Ann Whittingham.
ANN WHITTINGHAM . I live at Greenwich, and am a widow. The prisoner lodged at my house about a month with another woman—the hat and bonnet were lost from a cupboard in my front kitchen on the 30th of January—the policeman has them, but he is not here.
SUSAN CUNNINGHAM . I bought the hat and bonnet of the prisoner for 10 d.—she said it was her husband's hat, and her little girl's bonnet, who was dead, and she was in great distress.
NOT GUILTY .
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350202-643]
643. WILLIAM TINDELL was indicted for embezzlement.
JOHN JAMES BOWRING . I live in Laurence Pouteney-lane, and am a bottle-merchant. The prisoner was in my employ, and it was his duty to take and receive any sums of money—I sent him to Mrs. Brent's, at Dept-ford, for 1 l. 16 s.; and if he received it, it was his duty to pay it to me—he has not paid it me, nor any part of it—he returned, and stated that Mrs. Brent was ill, and she had nothing but a very large note in the house, and had not change to pay it; but she would send in a very few days.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. When was this? A. On the 14th of November—he continued in my service six or seven days afterwards—he had been with me six weeks or two months before—I know a person named Basan—he had a shipment about that time—I provided some bottles for that shipment—I received a turn of money on account—Mr. Bassan sent in some bottles himself, and I provided the rest—it was a shipment of ale and porter—it was seized, because there was an information that some of it had been put into old bottles, which cannot receive the draw-back—it was Mr. Basan's first shipment—he employed me to bottle the ale—Tindell did it under my superintendence—I received twenty-three gross of new bottles from Mr. Basan, and I had the money to buy the remaining two gross of new bottles—the ale is now in the cellar—I received the money before any bottles were bought—the reason all the ale was not in new bottles was, that part of it was bottled before the new bottles were sent in—I knew it ought to have been in new bottles, and I bought a gross of bottles myself, and therefore there could be but one gross of old; but the thing was settled with the Excise-office ten days ago, which was seven days before I knew the prisoner was in custody.
Q. Is it usual to give any entertainment to the men? A. It is usual in packing to give the packing officers their lunch—that was about the time the prisoner was sent to Deptford—I know nothing about the prisoner paying for an entertainment for the officers, I paid for their lunch every day—I heard of my wife's son taking a bottle of wine—it was returned the next day—it was Mr. Basan's wine—Tindell told me of it; but I look upon him in that transaction as guilty as the other; I do not prosecute the prisoner for that, but for taking this money—I did not send any of Mr. Basan's bottles to other people's houses instead of putting his ale into them, and if such a thing was done, it was without my knowledge—I heard that Thomas and the officers, and the prisoner, had been carousing together—Clancey told me of it—I did not know that he had been one of the party—he did not say he had rather pay the money than I should indict the prisoner—he condemned the prisoner all through—when we are bottling, there is always an Excise-officer, and it is customary to give them their lunch.
ALFRED BRENT . I am the ton of Mrs. Brent, of Deptford. I was at home, on the 14th of November, the principal part of the day—the prisoner came for the payment of a bill for two gallons of British brandy, which came to 1 l. 16 s.—my mother was ill in bed—my sister paid him in my presence, and he gave this receipt, signed, "For John James Bowring, William Tindell"—Mr. Bowring afterwards called for the money, and we showed him the receipt.
Cross-examined. Q. You say your mother was ill, perhaps she had a large note? A. Not that I know of—she might have one—my sister paid him two sovereigns in my presence; and, to the best of my knowledge, I gave the prisoner a pen and ink to write the receipt—I believe I saw him write it—I took it up directly after—I am sure this is the same paper.
Prisoner's Defence. I have no doubt this case would not have been urged against me, but for my being as evidence against other parties—he has sworn he was not aware that the ale was in old bottles, and he knows that old bottles were used; and in consequence of that, I have no doubt, this indictment is laid against me, which I was not aware of till last night—the whole sum of money was spent in a supper; as he had promised to give a leg of mutton supper, and to stand a few bottles of wine, provided the job was hushed up—he came home that night in a great passion, and accused Clancey of laying an indictment; and I took the officer to the ticket-porters' public-house to have a glass of brandy-and-water; Clancey and Thomas came to us, and we had ale and porter, and three glasses of brandy-and-water, to prevent any thing going forward against him—the officers then wanted supper, and we went to Shoreditch, and bought 8lbs. of tripe at Mr. Bennett's, and some onions; and we went to a house to have them—I was in liquor, and west to bed; and they had the tripe and the onions cooked—I had the whole money taken from me; sad since then, Clancey has said he would pay the money by instalments, rather than I should be indicted—Mr. Bowring has more than once pressed me to join him in partnership, knowing that my friends were respectable and would support him—I can produce an sight-years' character, and never was in any difficulty before; and it is, no doubt, to prevent my appearing against him that he has instituted this.
JOHN JAMES BOWRING re-examined. Q. Did you authorize him to have any leg of mutton supper, and to hush up matters with the officers? A. No, not in any way; the supper was—the same evening that he came home; but when he came in in the afternoon, I asked him for the money, before the supper; and, about six days afterwards, I was saying I thought it was a very extraordinary thing that Mrs. Brent did not send the money, as she was a lady who never required to be asked a second time—I then went out, and when I returned, the prisoner said there had been a messenger from Mrs. Brent, and she would send the next day—in a day or two afterwards I said I would go there myself—the prisoner then left, and I saw him no more.
MR. PAYNE. Q. Are you quite sure that you asked him for the money the same day as he received it? A. Yes; it was not the next day that I spoke to him about it—I will swear I did not give him authority in any way to do the best he could to prevent ray getting into any dilemma respecting these bottles—they could not get me into any scrape—they might Mr. Basan, I was not in the slightest degree amenable to the Excise.
( Witness for the Defence.)
ROBERT CLANCEY . I remember the dispute with the officer—I do not know when it was, but we had the tripe supper the same night—I did not have any brandy-and-water—the prisoner was the worse for drink—the officer said, it must be the prisoner or I who had laid the information—he and the prisoner went away together—the prisoner tried to pacify the officer—I had some supper with them—it was tripe—I believe the officer paid for it—the prisoner was there—I cannot tell where it was, but it was Hox-ton way—I cannot tell at what time the prisoner went to bed.
Prisoner. You are aware that Mr. Bowring told us to settle it as well as we could. Witness. No, I cannot say any thing about that—I am only a common working man.
(Joshua Holtham, a trunk-maker; Charles Thomas; and Edward Hollo-way, basket-maker, at Hammersmith, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
(See page 654.)

George Wilson and James Parker were capitally convicted. See page 515.
First Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350202-644]
644. JEREMIAH GROGAN was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of December, 1 pair of boots, value 5 s., the goods of John Franklin; and that he had before been convicted of felony.
JAMES FRANKLIN . I live in the Walworth-road, in the parish of St. Mary, Newington, in Surrey. About five or ten minutes before eight o'clock, on the morning of the 20th of December, the prisoner came and asked if we had any worsted shoe-laces—my father is a shoemaker, and his Christian name is John—I told him we had no worsted ones, we had silk and leather—he asked to see a pair of silk—I went to the top of the shop to get them, and as I returned, I saw him putting his hat on—he asked me what the laces came to—I said 2 d.—he said a half-penny was enough for them—I said I could not take it—he went out of the shop—I went out and watched him—I saw him join another person, about five doors from the shop—I followed, and when he got to the turning, he left the other, took his hat off, and ran with his hat in front of him—I followed, calling "Stop thief!"—he ran down a court, and came up with his hat on, and threatened to knock me down if I ran after him, but I followed him into the Kennington-road, met a policeman, and gave him in charge—I returned to the shop, and missed a pair of boots off a nail—he had run down Prospect-place—a man named Clark lives there—after being at the shop, we returned to the court, and met Clark's boy, who asked if we had lost any thing, and his father showed us the boots—there is a school-room in Williams'-court, which joins the houses in Prospect-place—I find my father's mark on the boots—they had been brought into the shop the night before.
Cross-examined by MR. CHAMBERS. Q. Are you quite certain the prisoner is the young man who came into the shop? A. Yes; he first went down Prospect-place, and then down Williams'-court—I was the only person in the shop—there were persons in the street when I went out—my father was not up—just as I went to pursue him, the shopwoman came
into the shop—I kept sight of him from the time he left the shop, till he came out of the court—while he ran down the court, I saw him at the end of Prospect-place, and when he turned round Williams'-court, I lost sight of him; and when I got up to Williams'-court, he was coming up, and put his hat on—young Clark did not come out till after he was secured—the other boy left him at the corner of Prospect-place.
JOSEPH CLARK . I live with my father in Prospect-place, Newington. He is a turner—on the morning of the 20th of December, I went into the yard of our house, and found a pair of boots—there is a school-room between our yard and Williams'-court—it is a low building—a person in Williams'-court could throw the boots over the school-room into our yard, easily—I took the boots in to my father—somebody told me they were in the yard, and I found them—my father delivered them to the constable, in my presence—I am quite sure they were the same.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you mean to say they never went out of your sight till you gave them to your father? A. Why, yes, I suppose for about five minutes—the wall of the school is in Williams'-court—there is no outer yard in Williams'-court—our yard is on the other side of the school-room from Williams'-court—they must have been thrown over the school-room—I found them about four feet from the school-room, which is about six feet broad—they were thrown about the middle of our yard—I could throw them over the school-room myself—a boy next door told me of it—he was in his master's garden.
WILLIAM SMITH ( police-constable L 4.) On the morning of the 20th of December, I was called to take the prisoner into custody—I met Joseph Clark—I went to his father's, and received a pair of boots from him, in his son's presence.
JAMES FRANKLIN re-examined. I know these boots to be my father's—they have his mark on them.
Cross-examined. Q. What is the mark? A. "F. 5."—we know the size—my father marks his boots in that way—I know them to be the boots which hung on the nail, by that mark—there were others marked the same, but there was not another pair hanging over the rail, of that size—we had sold no 5's that week, and these had only come in overnight.
PETER MCKEAND ( police-constable M 153.) I have a certificate of the prisoner's former conviction, which I got from Mr. Clark's office—I was present at the Town-hall, Borough, when the prisoner was tried and convicted—he is the person mentioned in the certificate ( read.)
Cross-examined. Q. How long have you known him? A. I never saw him before he was taken on that charge—I cannot say whether I have seen him since—I know his brother.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Transported for Seven Years.

645. JAMES NICHOLLS was indicted for an assault, with intent to commit an unnatural crime.

[trialAccount t18350202-646]
646. JOSEPH HEWITT was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of January, 1 handkerchief, value 3 s., the goods of Thomas Martin.
WILLIAM POTTER . I am an apprentice to Mr. Thomas Martin, of Tooley-street, Borough, a pawnbroker. On the 19th of January, in the afternoon, I was directed to the end of the shop by somebody, and saw the prisoner drawing his hand from his pocket, as if he had put something into
it, and then he looked at some other goods—I laid hold of him, and he immediately produced this handkerchief from his pocket—it had hung at up in the shop—I detained him till the officer came, and then gave him into custody.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. How long was it before the officer came? A. About ten minutes—I held him all the time—he did not tell me the handkerchief was falling, and he caught it to prevent its falling—a person at the door said, "What has he done?" and then he said, "The handkerchief fell down, and I picked it up." I had hold of him at the time the officer came—he did not attempt to escape—there was only one person in the shop, but four or five in the boxes—the person in the shop was a stranger—he could not see what he was doing, without turning round to look at him.
( Property produced and sworn to.)
Prisoner's Defence. I saw some yellow handkerchiefs hanging up, and went in and looked at than—I saw some more hanging near the counter—I went to look at those, and they came down in my hand—I said, "Here is one of your handkerchiefs come down"—he said, "I know all about it, you wanted to steal it"—I said, "I have not stolen it, and am not going to run away"—he said, "I will take good care you shall not," and he gave me in charge.
JURY to WILLIAM POTTER. Q. Was the handkerchief out of sight till it was given to you? A. Yes, his jacket was buttoned—I saw his hand withdrawn from his pocket, it had nothing in it—when I laid bold of him he gave me the handkerchief from his pocket.
JOHN STORY . I am an officer. I found the prisoner in the shop—Potter had the handkerchief in his hand—the prisoner was standing quietly there—he certainly denied it.
(Mary Guest—Thomas Hadlam, leather-dyer, Camberwell; and James Drew, leather-dyer; gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Confined Three Months.
Second Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350202-647]
647. WILLIAM PEACOCK and THOMAS FAIREY were indicted for stealing, on the 20th of January, 4 1/2 lbs. of pork, value 2 s. 6 d.; 3 1/2 lbs. of beef, value 1 s. 6 d.; and 1/2 lb. of suet, value 3 d.; the goods of George Putley, the master of the said William Peacock.
GEORGE PUTLEY . I live in High-street, Borough. Peacock was in my employ for about three months as foreman—in consequence of information I received I spoke to Hack and Wilkinson, and the prisoners were apprehended—Peacock was not authorized to sell on a Sunday—I do not open shop at all on a Sunday, at any time—if a regular customer sends, I may accommodate him, but then I expect to be applied to, before I allow him to serve—when I engaged him, he asked too much wages, and I said, "Remember, there is no Sunday work."
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. Has not he sometimes sold meat on a Sunday for you? A. Not to my knowledge—I never received money from him on Monday, for meat sold on Sunday, nor of any other serrant—I did not tell him I never sold meat on Sunday except on an emergency—I did not authorize him to sell on a Sunday except to my own regular customers—I swear I did not tell him he might sell to my regular customers on Sunday—I never gave him permission to serve any customers on Sunday—it has happened somtimes that a customer may have sent for
a piece of meat, if they had been going out and altered their mind, rather than disoblige them, I have allowed them to have it—I do not recollect that that has happened in his time—I believe persons have come for a mutton chop or a neck of mutton during his time.
Q. Did not you find on your desk 5 s., which he stated to you was payment for this meat? A. There was 5 s. on the desk on Sunday morning, and he told me it was payment for the meat which had been taken—he told me this in the shop—he pointed out the money to me—when I came down stairs, after hearing the bell ring, the officer was in the shop—I had two other shopmen—neither of them were there—he told me immediately on being charged with having stolen this—the price of the meat found was about 4 s. 3 d.—I believe Peacock's aunt has washed for him—there was some dirty linen found in the basket with the meat.
JOHN WILKINSON . I am inspector of the police. In consequence of information, I watched the shop on Sunday, the 25th of January, I was on the opposite side of the street, from between six and seven o'clock till half-past eight o'clock—I saw the prisoner Fairey come opposite Mr. Putley's shop just before it struck eight by the church clock—he stood near the curb, looking towards the door, till half-past eight o'clock—he waited there half an hour—he neither rung nor knocked at the door—I observed Peacock open the door—he popped out, and took a hat-box, which Fairey had in his hand, and a basket—he turned into the shop, and was out again in a moment—he handed the basket to Fairey, who walked as fast as he could down towards London-bridge, till he came to Chapel-court—he turned up Chapel-court, and I apprehended him—I laid hold of the basket, and said, "It is all right"—I said to the constable behind me, "Take this man in charge"—I found a belly of pork very wet, as if it had just come from the pickling-tub, a piece of beef, and a piece of suet—I sent him to the station-house, and went directly to Mr. Putley's house—I rang the bell four times before it was answered, and then Peacock opened the door—I said, "We want you, I must see Mr. Putley directly"—he said he bad sold the meat—I had told him I wanted him for robbing his master—I said, "If you have sold it, what does it weigh?"—he said, "It weighed ten pounds"—that be had sold it at 6 d. per lb. and there was the money on the desk—there was half-a-crown, two shillings, and a sixpence on the desk—I looked at the scale, and it was quite dry and clean—the prosecutor came down directly, and I took Peacock into custody—at the watch-house, Fairey said the meat was paid for—I asked him how much—he said, it weighed seven pounds, and he had given half-a-crown for it.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there not a cloth in the basket? A. There was a butcher's woollen apron—he said at Union Hall next day, that he put the meat on the apron to be weighed, that it might not dirty the scale, but he did not say so at the station-house—I had not dirty the scale, of the meat to him, as weighing seven pounds—he said, "The meat"—Putley was in the shop all the time I was questioning Peacock, and must have heard all, I should think.
COURT. Q. How were the articles placed in the basket—was the meat uppermost? A. No—the bundle of linen was on the top of the meat—then there was the pork, with the blue apron wrapped round it, and the beef and suet were under that, at the bottom of the basket—I saw the meat as soon as I laid hold of the basket, and pulled it open—it was a flag basket.
WILLIAM ROSS . I am a policeman. I was with Wilkinson, and took Fairey to the station-house.
WILLIAM NYE . I know Fairey by sight—I had seen him near the prosecutor's shop several times at the door, the beginning of December last, and gave information to Mr. Putley.
Peacock's Defence. When the prisoner came to me on Sunday morning, I gave him the basket, with my linen in it—he said he wanted some meat—I weighed this meat, and made it ten pounds—I put the apron and cloth into the scale—I gave him the meat—he gave me 2 s. 6 d.—I said it was not enough—he said, "You owe me 7 s. for washing, and whatever there is to pay, you must settle it"—I put the half crown on the desk, with 2 s. 6 d. more, which the meat came to—I told him to go to the Blue Mead tap, and I would come to him, and pay him what I owed him for my washing.
JOHN WILKINSON re-examined. The door was opened to me in about two minutes—Fairey had got the door of the Blue Mead tap in his hand, when I took hold of him—when I got into the prosecutor's house, the maid-servant was at the foot of the stain—I found a watch and some money in Peacock's box.
MARY WEBB . I am the servant. I heard the bell ring—the prisoner was in the kitchen when it rang, and went down to answer it, but he did not go till it rang three times—he went down the first time it rang—it rang three times more, and then I went down, as he did not open it.
Cross-examined. Q. Do you know Fairey was in the habit of coming for the dirty linen? A. The bell never rang in the morning, and I never saw any body come for his dirty linen.
Fairey's Defence. I went to the shop for his linen—sometimes I ring at the bell, and sometimes I do not ring—when he came out, I have said to him, "If you have a bit of meat to suit me, put it in, that will do instead of the washing"—I said that morning, "Seven or eight pounds will do for me this morning"—he put this in—I gave him 2 s. 6 d.—he said it was not enough—I said, "You pay the difference, and I will settle it when you come to me"—I was going into the public-house door, when the sergeant of the police caught hold of me, and said, "I have been looking for you a good while, I want what you have in that basket"—I said, "What I have is my own, I have paid for it"—he said to the policeman, "You take him, I will go after the other"—at the station-house, he asked how much the meat came to, and I said, I gave half-a-crown for it.
(Moses Woolf, of Rosemary-lane; John Glanville, painter and glazier, Hackney-road; and Ann Noakes, Green Gate-gardens, Hackney-road, deposed to the prisoner Fairey's good character; and Charles Cook, butcher, Red Lion-street, Holborn; and Andrew Curry, boot-maker, Phoenix-street, to that of Peacock.)
PEACOCK. GUILTY .—Aged 23.
FAIREY. GUILTY .—Aged 52.
Recommended to mercy on account of their previous good character.— Confined Twelve Months.
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350202-648]
648. JOHN MASON was indicted for stealing, on the 8th of January, 1 £50 Bank-note, the property of James Wilson, his master.
GEORGE GRAY . I am clerk to James Wilson, a hat-manufacturer, living in Holland-street, Blackfriars-road. The prisoner had been his
errand-boy for two weeks—I received a 50 l. Bank-note from Drewett and Co., No. 13,789, dated the 19th of November, 1834—it was Mr. Wilson's money—I gave it to him, and he enclosed it in a letter, and sealed it, but nobody saw him do it—it was on Thursday, the 8th of January—I entered the note in the cash-book—Mr. Wilson, that evening, gave me a sealed letter, which I suppose contained that note—it was directed to Mr. John H. How, to the care of John Wilson and Co., hat-manufacturers, Denton, Lancashire—I directed the prisoner to take care of it, as it was valuable, and contained money, and to be sure and put it into the post-office—on the Saturday, I was at Mrs. Colston's, and she showed me a 50 l. note, which I knew to be the note I mentioned—Mr. Wilson is in town, but not in attendance—I have not a doubt of the note being the same—the prisoner came back that day, and remained till Saturday, and then left without notice—he was taken at the Northumberland Arms.
MARY COLSTON . I keep the Northumberland Arms, Holland-street, Blackfriars-road. The prisoner came to me on the 8th of January for change for this 50 l. note—he said he came from Mr. Wilson's, and said, "Please to give Mr. Gray change for a 50 l. note"—I had given him change the Saturday before for the firm, and knowing he lived there, I gave him thirty sovereigns, a 10 l. note in two halves, and two 5 l. notes—I had changed the same 10 l. note for Mr. Gray the Saturday before, and the prisoner had brought it—I told him to tell Mr. Gray it was the note I had received from him on Saturday—I have the 50 l. note now.
GEORGE GRAY re-examined. This is the same note—he was apprehended on the 13th of January—we have recovered 4 l. 2 s. 6 d., and an old watch.
JOHN RIGBY . I am a hatter, and work for Mr. Wilson. I saw the prisoner come into the public-house where I was, and ask for change for a £50 note for Mr. Gray—the landlady went up stairs, brought the change down, gave it him, and told him to tell Gray the £10 note was the one she had received from him on the Saturday before—he said "Very well"—he went out with the money in his hand, and before he went out he said, "If I should run away with this money, what a funk they would be in"—he then walked out.
(Thomas Hacker, hat-dyer, Union-street, Borough; and John Streatham, a hatter, gave the prisoner a good character.)
GUILTY . Aged 18.— Transported for Seven Years.
Second Jury, before Mr. Sergeant Arabin.
[trialAccount t18350202-649]
649. JOHN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 30th of January, 3 planes, value 5 s.; and 5 chisels, value 2 s.; the goods of Joseph Dowden; 1 gauge, value 1 s.; the goods of Richard Ledger; and 1 plane, value 1 s.; the goods of Richard Ockerford.
WILLIAM COTTLE . I am a policeman. On the 30th of January, at a quarter before two o'clock in the morning, I was in Lant-street, Borough, and saw the prisoner carrying something—he popped round the corner—I said to him, "What have you here?"—he said, "My tools"—I said, "What are you?"—he said, "Abricklayer"—I said, "A bricklayer does not use carpenter's tools"—he said, "Well, it is all right"—I said, "I do not know that, I shall take you to the station-house"—as I took him there he said, "Well, I have had nothing to do for two or three months—I shall have something to do now"—he was three-quarters of a mile from the building they were taken from.
JOSEPH DOWDEN . I am a carpenter. Some of these tools are mine—I left them in an unfinished house in Tooley-street—I know nothing of the prisoner.
RICHARD LEDGER . I worked at the building—this mortice gauge is mine.
RICHARD OCKERFORD . I lost thete planes from the same building.
Prisoner's Defence. I was hired to carry them by a man in the Borough, who had a large basket full of them—I was to meet him at the Bricklayer's Arms—I did not know where they came from.
GUILTY . Aged 40.— Transported for Seven Years.
Fourth Jury, before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18350202-650]
650. JOHN JUSTICE and WILLIAM PALMER were indicted for stealing, on the 4th of February, at St. Mary, Newington, 26lbs. weight of soap, value 8 s.; and 21lbs., weight of candles; the goods of Jabez Pike, the master of John Justice; and WILLIAM MILLER was indicted for feloniously receiving 12lbs. weight of soap, value 4 s. 6 d.; and 21lbs. weight of candles, value 8 s., part of the said goods, well knowing them to be stolen, as aforesaid.—2nd COUNT, for receiving of an evil disposed person.
JABEZ PIKE . I am an oil and colourman. Justice was in my employ, as porter—I sent him on errands to deliver goods—Palmer was the servant of a Mr. Holraan, my neighbour—my premises open upon Holman's yard—on the night of the 4th of February, I received information from Holman, and accompanied him to a cart-shed in his yard; and under the cart, in a box, I saw a quantity of soap, which I had every reason to believe was mine, from having a similar description of goods on my premises—Holman went into the loft, and there was a quantity of candles in a canvass bag—I have every reason to believe they were mine, having candles of a similar description on my premises—I got a policeman, and went to Holman's premises again, and in the interval the property had been removed—the candles from the loft, and the soap from the box—I found near the box five bars of soap afterwards in a nose-bag—in consequence of information we went to a shop in Arbour-street, within fifty yards of my premises, and there saw Miller—I told him I had come for the soap and candles which Holman's boy had left a few minutes before—his reply was, "I have no candles"—I said, "It is no use to deny it; we know all about it;" and then he stooped down, opened a drawer, and produced the soap, and afterwards the candles, in a canvass bag—I believe the bag to be the same as I had seen in Holman's loft—I got a policeman, and gave Justice and Palmer into custody—I value the candles at 8 s., and the soap at 8 s. or 9 s.—Miller keeps a chandler's shop—Holman is a carter.
Cross-examined by MR. PHILLIPS. Q. I believe you stated to Miller that what you wanted was some soap and candles, which Holman's boy had brought? A. Yes—the prisoners were all before the magistrate—Justice and Palmer were detained—I undertook Miller should come next day, and he was let go on that—he has been a neighbour of mine many years—he came according to promise next day—I offered the magistrate to become bail for him—the magistrate said as the Grand Jury were sitting, it was not worth while—I have supplied Miller with goods, but not for months past.
JAMES BOOTH ( policeman N 99.) I was on duty between nine and ten o'clock at night on the 4th of February, in Newington-causeway—Mr. Holman fetched me to his premises—I went with him and the prosecutor,
and saw Palmer—Holman said, "Charles, the policeman wants you concerning some soap and candles"—he said, "Master, I do not know any thing about soap and candles"—he said, "You do, for there is soap and candles deposited on my premises unknown to me, and they have no business there"—I took him into custody—Mr. Holman immediately said there was some soap in a box in the cart-shed, and candles in a bag in the loft—Holman went to the loft, and found the candles were removed—I afterwards went with the prosecutor to the cart-shed, and the soap was also removed—Palmer said he did not know any thing about them—the prosecutor stated that he did know, and, if in case they were not there, he knew where they were, from information he received—I went to Pike's house, and took Justice into custody, and went from there to Miller's—Pike said, "Mr. Miller, I have come for the soap and candles which have been brought here"—Mr. Miller said, "What soap and candles?"—he said, "The soap and candles brought by Holman's boy"—he hesitated a moment, then opened a drawer, and brought out four bars of soap, and then the bag of candles, and laid them on the counter—I took possession of them, and took them to the station-house with Palmer and Justice in my custody—I went back to Miller's house, and fetched him to the station-house—I then went and examined Holman's yard, and found five bars of soap under a cart-shed, behind a large piece of board—Palmer had said, in my presence, that there was more soap in a nose-bag in the cart-shed—I found that—he said he had taken the candles and soap to Miller's shop, and stated to Miller that he should call for them that evening.
CHARLES FEARON . I am a carpenter, and live in George-street. I was at the prosecutor's house—in consequence of what he said to me, I remained in the parlour with Justice—I said what a shocking thing it was for a young lad like him, and what a shocking thing it would be for his father and mother, and if he was a lad of mine, it would almost break my heart—he burst into tears, and said, "What will my father and mother say?"—he stated to me that he had taken about thirty-four or thirty-five cakes of soap, and about two dozen candles, from his master.
JABEZ PIKE re-examined. I have every reason to believe this is my soap—I have a great deal of it at home—I am more positive of the candles—there are certain candles there which I know—one of them is a candle eight to the pound, a long description of candle, which I know well to be mine—I did not miss any, our stock is too large.
Palmer's Defence. Justice called me out in the morning, and said he had some soap, if I was of a mind to take it—I went up the yard, just opened the gate, and he gave it into my hand.
Miller's Defence. Palmer brought the goods to my house, and asked me to let him leave them till he called for them.
(Several respectable witnesses gave Miller a good character.)
JUSTICE— GUILTY . Aged 16.
PALMER— GUILTY . Aged 16.
MILLER- NOT GUILTY .
Confined Six Months.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.

JOHN VOISON was indicted for stealing, on the 14th of January, 28lbs. of leaden pipe, value 5 s., the goods of William Lawrence; 1 square, value 9 d., the goods of John Redway; 1 plumb-bob, value 3 d., and 1 spoke-shave, value 6 d., the goods of William Hummerston; and 1 jacket, value 1 s. 6 d., the goods of John Clarke; to which he pleaded
GUILTY . Aged 35.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18350202-652]
652. FREDERICK KNOWLDEN was indicted for embezzlement.
MILES POWELL . I keep a retail beer-shop in Minerva-terrace, North-Brixton. The prisoner was my pot-boy, and it was his duty to receive money, and to account to me for it—we took the score of him twice every day—he never paid me 1 s. 4 d. received of Mrs. Smith on the 27th of December.
Prisoner. There was 3 s. due to me. Witness. I was to give him 3 s. a week—he had been two or three weeks with me, and I had paid him, but this time he left me on the Saturday, and his week was not due till the Monday—he received more than 3 s. of the customers that day, and he absconded.
MARY ANN SMITH . I have beer from the prosecutor—I paid the prisoner 1 s. 4 d., on the 27th of December, for his master.
The prisoner pleaded poverty.
GUILTY . Aged 19.— Recommended to mercy by the Jury and the
Prosecutor.— Confined Seven Days.
Fifth Jury, before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18350202-653]

635. NICHOLAS HAMILTON was indicted for stealing, on the 26th of January, 2 fowls, value 4 s., the goods of George Winter.
GEORGE WINTER . I live at No. 5, Minerva-terrace, New-cross. I had some fowls—I saw them safe on the 25th of January, about mid-day, and on the 26th my fowl-house was broken open, and I missed two Poland fowls—these are similar to them.
SAMUEL BARKER ( police-constable R 203.) I met the prisoner on the road, between four and five o'clock that day—I saw he was rather bulky, and asked what he had under his jacket—he said, "Nothing but another shirt"—I put my hand to his side, and said there was something more than that—he said, "Yes, a fowl—my sister, who lives at Greenwich, gave it me"—I asked where his sister lived—he said he did not know—he then said, "Where the fair is kept, and her name is Anderson"—I said, "Let me look at the fowl"—he did, and then I found the other on him—this was half a mile from the prosecutor's—I took the prisoner to the station-house, and then to the cage—I found the prosecutor, and I went to the prisoner, after he had been remanded—I asked him how he came to find such a curious place as where the fowls were—he then said that Captain Wilson's son told him where they were, and he wished to be lagged for seven years, for he should be better out of the country than in it.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Transported for Seven Years.
